Well, I guess what brings me in is… since I stopped working, I just can’t seem to slow down. I’m up and moving all day, going from one little job to the next, like there’s still a crew waiting on me or something.

Unusually upbeat… I don’t know that I’d say that. I mean, sometimes I feel good if I’m getting things done, like if the shed finally looks halfway decent, that’s satisfying. But excited, like really wound up happy? I’m not sure.

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d call it unwanted thoughts exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ll see something sitting out and it kind of nags at me, like, “Well, I should put that away,” and then that turns into three other things.

But a strong sense that I have to… I don’t know. It’s not like a voice in my head or some terrible thought I’m trying to get rid of. It’s more that I notice one thing, then another, and I’m already up anyway. Like yesterday I went to take out the trash and ended up checking the side gate, then looking at the hose, and then I remembered this time years ago when we had a leak behind the garage—sorry, I’m wandering.

So… I guess I haven’t really noticed it being because of unwanted thoughts. It’s more just, I get going and don’t stop.

Worrying a lot… I don’t know. I mean, I worry about the normal stuff, I guess—bills, whether I remembered to lock the door, if the car’s making some noise it shouldn’t. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying all day for no reason.

I mean, I’m up and moving a lot, but I haven’t paid close attention to whether I’m actually sleeping less. Some nights I’m in bed and my mind’s kind of… well, I start thinking about the garage or the yard, and then I remember this old job I had where we’d be up before dawn—but that’s not exactly what you’re asking, is it?\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’ve noticed needing less sleep, not clearly.\n\nDoctor: When you get into this go-go mode, do you also feel unusually upbeat or excited?\n\nPatient: Unusually upbeat… I don’t know that I’d say that. I mean, sometimes I feel good if I’m getting things done, like if the shed finally looks halfway decent, that’s satisfying. But excited, like really wound up happy? I’m not sure.\n\nIt’s more like I just keep moving. My wife used to say I’d pace around even when there wasn’t much to do, and I’d tell her, well, there’s always something—though half the time I’d end up reorganizing a drawer instead of finishing what I started. But as far as feeling especially upbeat, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: When you keep busy like this, do you feel driven to do it because unwanted thoughts or a strong sense that you have to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it unwanted thoughts exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ll see something sitting out and it kind of nags at me, like, “Well, I should put that away,” and then that turns into three other things.\n\nBut a strong sense that I have to… I don’t know. It’s not like a voice in my head or some terrible thought I’m trying to get rid of. It’s more that I notice one thing, then another, and I’m already up anyway. Like yesterday I went to take out the trash and ended up checking the side gate, then looking at the hose, and then I remembered this time years ago when we had a leak behind the garage—sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed it being because of unwanted thoughts. It’s more just, I get going and don’t stop.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, were you already the kind of person who got distracted easily and jumped from one task to another?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Even in grade school, I remember teachers saying I was bright, but kind of scattered. I’d lose pencils all the time—my mother used to get annoyed because she’d buy those big packs, and somehow they’d just disappear.\n\nAnd I’d call out answers, too, before I was supposed to. Then I’d need reminders to finish my work before recess, because I’d start one thing and then be looking out the window or messing with something in my desk. So… yes, I guess that was already there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying a lot about everyday things, even when there isn’t a clear reason to?\n\nPatient: Worrying a lot… I don’t know. I mean, I worry about the normal stuff, I guess—bills, whether I remembered to lock the door, if the car’s making some noise it shouldn’t. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying all day for no reason.\n\nSometimes something will get in my head and I’ll circle back to it, like if I need to call someone and then I forget, then remember it while I’m doing something else. Actually, that happened with the insurance papers once—well, not important.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of myself as a big worrier, no.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

On guard… hmm. I don’t think so, not really. I mean, if I hear a noise outside at night I’ll notice it, but I think most people would. It’s not like I’m moving around because I think something bad is about to happen.

It’s more like I just get up to do one thing, and then—well, like the other day I went to check if the back door was shut, and then I saw the mail on the table, and then I remembered I hadn’t watered the plant by the window. That plant’s been half-dead for years anyway, my wife used to say I either drowned it or forgot it completely.

But no, I don’t think I feel alert for danger exactly. I haven’t really noticed that.

like this, do you feel on guard, like you need to stay alert for something bad to happen?\n\nPatient: On guard… hmm. I don’t think so, not really. I mean, if I hear a noise outside at night I’ll notice it, but I think most people would. It’s not like I’m moving around because I think something bad is about to happen.\n\nIt’s more like I just get up to do one thing, and then—well, like the other day I went to check if the back door was shut, and then I saw the mail on the table, and then I remembered I hadn’t watered the plant by the window. That plant’s been half-dead for years anyway, my wife used to say I either drowned it or forgot it completely.\n\nBut no, I don’t think I feel alert for danger exactly. I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Did this getting-up-and-going all day become noticeably worse after you stopped working?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say that’s when it really got noticeable. When I was working, I was always moving anyway, so it just seemed normal—like there was always another job to get to, somebody needing something, that sort of thing.\n\nBut since I stopped working, I still can’t really slow down. I’m up and down all day, going from one little thing to the next, like there’s still a crew waiting on me or I’m supposed to be somewhere. I’ll start to sit, then think of something in the garage, then the trash, then some little thing in the kitchen… it just keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Did anything traumatic happen around the time you stopped working?\n\nPatient: Traumatic? It was more just a change. I remember cleaning out my locker and finding old receipts and a pair of gloves I thought I’d lost, and then I brought all that junk home and it sat by the garage door for weeks.\n\nSo, no, not that I can think of.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave being easily distracted and jumping from one task to another caused problems for you in daily life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it has, I suppose. I don’t like saying that, but yes.

My wife ended up sort of taking over the calendar, because I’d say I was going to be somewhere and then I’d get caught up doing something else, or I’d start one thing and forget the other thing completely. She checks behind me more than I’d like to admit.

And I’ve had to apologize to the community center and the garden group a few times, too. I meant to bring something, or show up for something, and then… well, I didn’t follow through. The garden group had this whole thing with mulch one Saturday—funny how much people can argue about mulch—but anyway, yes, I let people down more than once.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what brings me in is… since I stopped working, I just can’t seem to slow down. I’m up and moving all day, going from one little job to the next, like there’s still a crew waiting on me or something.\n\nI’ll start one thing, then notice something else that needs doing, and before I know it I’ve been on my feet all day. Used to be, that kind of pace made sense at work, you know? There was always somebody needing something, always another task. Now it’s just me at home, but my body still acts like I’ve got to keep moving.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in this go-go mode, do you find that you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’m up and moving a lot, but I haven’t paid close attention to whether I’m actually sleeping less. Some nights I’m in bed and my mind’s kind of… well, I start thinking about the garage or the yard, and then I remember this old job I had where we’d be up before dawn—but that’s not exactly what you’re asking, is it?\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’ve noticed needing less sleep, not clearly.\n\nDoctor: When you get into this go-go mode, do you also feel unusually upbeat or excited?\n\nPatient: Unusually upbeat… I don’t know that I’d say that. I mean, sometimes I feel good if I’m getting things done, like if the shed finally looks halfway decent, that’s satisfying. But excited, like really wound up happy? I’m not sure.\n\nIt’s more like I just keep moving. My wife used to say I’d pace around even when there wasn’t much to do, and I’d tell her, well, there’s always something—though half the time I’d end up reorganizing a drawer instead of finishing what I started. But as far as feeling especially upbeat, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: When you keep busy like this, do you feel driven to do it because unwanted thoughts or a strong sense that you have to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it unwanted thoughts exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ll see something sitting out and it kind of nags at me, like, “Well, I should put that away,” and then that turns into three other things.\n\nBut a strong sense that I have to… I don’t know. It’s not like a voice in my head or some terrible thought I’m trying to get rid of. It’s more that I notice one thing, then another, and I’m already up anyway. Like yesterday I went to take out the trash and ended up checking the side gate, then looking at the hose, and then I remembered this time years ago when we had a leak behind the garage—sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed it being because of unwanted thoughts. It’s more just, I get going and don’t stop.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, were you already the kind of person who got distracted easily and jumped from one task to another?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Then I’d need reminders to finish my work before recess, because I’d start one thing and then be looking out the window or messing with something in my desk. I mean, I worry about the normal stuff, I guess—bills, whether I remembered to lock the door, if the car’s making some noise it shouldn’t. Yes, I do. Even if I’m trying to relax, like sitting through a quiet meeting or one of those church talks, I start feeling like I need to move around.

I’ll shift in the seat, tap my foot, stretch my legs out, then pull them back in… that sort of thing. Sometimes I’m thinking, “Just sit still, you’re a grown man,” but then my back feels tight or my knee starts bouncing. Church pews don’t help either—never built for comfort, if you ask me.

But yes, even when I know I’m supposed to stay put, I get that feeling like I ought to get up and stretch or move around.

Used to be, that kind of pace made sense at work, you know? There was always somebody needing something, always another task. Now it’s just me at home, but my body still acts like I’ve got to keep moving.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in this go-go mode, do you find that you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’m up and moving a lot, but I haven’t paid close attention to whether I’m actually sleeping less. Some nights I’m in bed and my mind’s kind of… well, I start thinking about the garage or the yard, and then I remember this old job I had where we’d be up before dawn—but that’s not exactly what you’re asking, is it?\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’ve noticed needing less sleep, not clearly.\n\nDoctor: When you get into this go-go mode, do you also feel unusually upbeat or excited?\n\nPatient: Unusually upbeat… I don’t know that I’d say that. I’m not sure.\n\nIt’s more like I just keep moving. But as far as feeling especially upbeat, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: When you keep busy like this, do you feel driven to do it because unwanted thoughts or a strong sense that you have to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it unwanted thoughts exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ll see something sitting out and it kind of nags at me, like, “Well, I should put that away,” and then that turns into three other things.\n\nBut a strong sense that I have to… I don’t know. It’s not like a voice in my head or some terrible thought I’m trying to get rid of. It’s more that I notice one thing, then another, and I’m already up anyway. Like yesterday I went to take out the trash and ended up checking the side gate, then looking at the hose, and then I remembered this time years ago when we had a leak behind the garage—sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed it being because of unwanted thoughts. It’s more just, I get going and don’t stop.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, were you already the kind of person who got distracted easily and jumped from one task to another?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I’m up and down all day, going from one little thing to the next, like there’s still a crew waiting on me or I’m supposed to be somewhere. I’ll start to sit, then think of something in the garage, then the trash, then some little thing in the kitchen… it just keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Did anything traumatic happen around the time you stopped working?\n\nPatient: Traumatic? It was more just a change. I remember cleaning out my locker and finding old receipts and a pair of gloves I thought I’d lost, and then I brought all that junk home and it sat by the garage door for weeks.\n\nSo, no, not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Have being easily distracted and jumping from one task to another caused problems for you in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I suppose. I don’t like saying that, but yes.\n\nMy wife ended up sort of taking over the calendar, because I’d say I was going to be somewhere and then I’d get caught up doing something else, or I’d start one thing and forget the other thing completely. She checks behind me more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAnd I’ve had to apologize to the community center and the garden group a few times, too. I meant to bring something, or show up for something, and then… well, I didn’t follow through. The garden group had this whole thing with mulch one Saturday—funny how much people can argue about mulch—but anyway, yes, I let people down more than once.\n\nDoctor: Even when you try to relax, do you feel restless or unable to stay seated for very long?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even if I’m trying to relax, like sitting through a quiet meeting or one of those church talks, I start feeling like I need to move around.\n\nI’ll shift in the seat, tap my foot, stretch my legs out, then pull them back in… that sort of thing. Sometimes I’m thinking, “Just sit still, you’re a grown man,” but then my back feels tight or my knee starts bouncing. Church pews don’t help either—never built for comfort, if you ask me.\n\nBut yes, even when I know I’m supposed to stay put, I get that feeling like I ought to get up and stretch or move around.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s more just, I get going and don’t stop.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, were you already the kind of person who got distracted easily and jumped from one task to another?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Even in grade school, I remember teachers saying I was bright, but kind of scattered. I’d lose pencils all the time—my mother used to get annoyed because she’d buy those big packs, and somehow they’d just disappear.\n\nAnd I’d call out answers, too, before I was supposed to. Then I’d need reminders to finish my work before recess, because I’d start one thing and then be looking out the window or messing with something in my desk. So… yes, I guess that was already there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying a lot about everyday things, even when there isn’t a clear reason to?\n\nPatient: Worrying a lot… I don’t know. I mean, I worry about the normal stuff, I guess—bills, whether I remembered to lock the door, if the car’s making some noise it shouldn’t. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying all day for no reason.\n\nSometimes something will get in my head and I’ll circle back to it, like if I need to call someone and then I forget, then remember it while I’m doing something else. Actually, that happened with the insurance papers once—well, not important.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of myself as a big worrier, no.\n\nDoctor: When you keep moving like this, do you feel on guard, like you need to stay alert for something bad to happen?\n\nPatient: On guard… hmm. I don’t think so, not really. I mean, if I hear a noise outside at night I’ll notice it, but I think most people would. It’s not like I’m moving around because I think something bad is about to happen.\n\nIt’s more like I just get up to do one thing, and then—well, like the other day I went to check if the back door was shut, and then I saw the mail on the table, and then I remembered I hadn’t watered the plant by the window. That plant’s been half-dead for years anyway, my wife used to say I either drowned it or forgot it completely.\n\nBut no, I don’t think I feel alert for danger exactly. I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Did this getting-up-and-going all day become noticeably worse after you stopped working?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say that’s when it really got noticeable. When I was working, I was always moving anyway, so it just seemed normal—like there was always another job to get to, somebody needing something, that sort of thing.\n\nBut since I stopped working, I still can’t really slow down. I’m up and down all day, going from one little thing to the next, like there’s still a crew waiting on me or I’m supposed to be somewhere. I’ll start to sit, then think of something in the garage, then the trash, then some little thing in the kitchen… it just keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Did anything traumatic happen around the time you stopped working?\n\nPatient: Traumatic? No, I don’t think so. I mean, stopping work was strange, sure—after doing it for so many years, suddenly your day doesn’t have the same shape to it.\n\nBut nothing like an accident or… I don’t know, something terrible happening right then. It was more just a change. I remember cleaning out my locker and finding old receipts and a pair of gloves I thought I’d lost, and then I brought all that junk home and it sat by the garage door for weeks.\n\nSo, no, not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Have being easily distracted and jumping from one task to another caused problems for you in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I suppose. I don’t like saying that, but yes.\n\nMy wife ended up sort of taking over the calendar, because I’d say I was going to be somewhere and then I’d get caught up doing something else, or I’d start one thing and forget the other thing completely. She checks behind me more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAnd I’ve had to apologize to the community center and the garden group a few times, too. I meant to bring something, or show up for something, and then… well, I didn’t follow through. The garden group had this whole thing with mulch one Saturday—funny how much people can argue about mulch—but anyway, yes, I let people down more than once.\n\nDoctor: Even when you try to relax, do you feel restless or unable to stay seated for very long?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even if I’m trying to relax, like sitting through a quiet meeting or one of those church talks, I start feeling like I need to move around.\n\nI’ll shift in the seat, tap my foot, stretch my legs out, then pull them back in… that sort of thing. Sometimes I’m thinking, “Just sit still, you’re a grown man,” but then my back feels tight or my knee starts bouncing. Church pews don’t help either—never built for comfort, if you ask me.\n\nBut yes, even when I know I’m supposed to stay put, I get that feeling like I ought to get up and stretch or move around.\n\nDoctor: When you get like this, do other people tell you that you’re talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. My wife might say something if I get going, but I don’t know if that’s “more than usual” or just me telling a story badly.\n\nI mean, some people tell stories straight from A to B, and I’ll remember something about a neighbor’s dog or what happened at the hardware store, and then I’ve taken the scenic route. But as far as people saying, “You’re talking too much today,” no… I don’t really recall that being a regular thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

That plant’s been half-dead for years anyway, my wife used to say I either drowned it or forgot it completely.\n\nBut no, I don’t think I feel alert for danger exactly. I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Did this getting-up-and-going all day become noticeably worse after you stopped working?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say that’s when it really got noticeable. When I was working, I was always moving anyway, so it just seemed normal—like there was always another job to get to, somebody needing something, that sort of thing.\n\nBut since I stopped working, I still can’t really slow down. I’m up and down all day, going from one little thing to the next, like there’s still a crew waiting on me or I’m supposed to be somewhere. I’ll start to sit, then think of something in the garage, then the trash, then some little thing in the kitchen… it just keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Did anything traumatic happen around the time you stopped working?\n\nPatient: Traumatic? No, I don’t think so. Even if I’m trying to relax, like sitting through a quiet meeting or one of those church talks, I start feeling like I need to move around.\n\nI’ll shift in the seat, tap my foot, stretch my legs out, then pull them back in… that sort of thing. Sometimes I’m thinking, “Just sit still, you’re a grown man,” but then my back feels tight or my knee starts bouncing. But as far as people saying, “You’re talking too much today,” no… I don’t really recall that being a regular thing.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually confident, like you could do things better than other people or take on more than was realistic?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I’ve had moments where I thought, “Oh, I can handle that,” and then it turned out to be more of a project than I expected.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F41.1, F42.8, F43.8, F31.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what brings me in is… since I stopped working, I just can’t seem to slow down. I’m up and moving all day, going from one little job to the next, like there’s still a crew waiting on me or something.\n\nI’ll start one thing, then notice something else that needs doing, and before I know it I’ve been on my feet all day. Used to be, that kind of pace made sense at work, you know? There was always somebody needing something, always another task. Now it’s just me at home, but my body still acts like I’ve got to keep moving.\n\nDoctor: When you’re in this go-go mode, do you find that you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’m up and moving a lot, but I haven’t paid close attention to whether I’m actually sleeping less. Some nights I’m in bed and my mind’s kind of… well, I start thinking about the garage or the yard, and then I remember this old job I had where we’d be up before dawn—but that’s not exactly what you’re asking, is it?\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’ve noticed needing less sleep, not clearly.\n\nDoctor: When you get into this go-go mode, do you also feel unusually upbeat or excited?\n\nPatient: Unusually upbeat… I don’t know that I’d say that. I mean, sometimes I feel good if I’m getting things done, like if the shed finally looks halfway decent, that’s satisfying. But excited, like really wound up happy? I’m not sure.\n\nIt’s more like I just keep moving. My wife used to say I’d pace around even when there wasn’t much to do, and I’d tell her, well, there’s always something—though half the time I’d end up reorganizing a drawer instead of finishing what I started. But as far as feeling especially upbeat, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: When you keep busy like this, do you feel driven to do it because unwanted thoughts or a strong sense that you have to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it unwanted thoughts exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ll see something sitting out and it kind of nags at me, like, “Well, I should put that away,” and then that turns into three other things.\n\nBut a strong sense that I have to… I don’t know. It’s not like a voice in my head or some terrible thought I’m trying to get rid of. It’s more that I notice one thing, then another, and I’m already up anyway. Like yesterday I went to take out the trash and ended up checking the side gate, then looking at the hose, and then I remembered this time years ago when we had a leak behind the garage—sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nSo… I guess I haven’t really noticed it being because of unwanted thoughts. It’s more just, I get going and don’t stop.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, were you already the kind of person who got distracted easily and jumped from one task to another?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Even in grade school, I remember teachers saying I was bright, but kind of scattered. I’d lose pencils all the time—my mother used to get annoyed because she’d buy those big packs, and somehow they’d just disappear.\n\nAnd I’d call out answers, too, before I was supposed to. Then I’d need reminders to finish my work before recess, because I’d start one thing and then be looking out the window or messing with something in my desk. So… yes, I guess that was already there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying a lot about everyday things, even when there isn’t a clear reason to?\n\nPatient: Worrying a lot… I don’t know. I mean, I worry about the normal stuff, I guess—bills, whether I remembered to lock the door, if the car’s making some noise it shouldn’t. But I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around worrying all day for no reason.\n\nSometimes something will get in my head and I’ll circle back to it, like if I need to call someone and then I forget, then remember it while I’m doing something else. Actually, that happened with the insurance papers once—well, not important.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of myself as a big worrier, no.\n\nDoctor: When you keep moving like this, do you feel on guard, like you need to stay alert for something bad to happen?\n\nPatient: On guard… hmm. I don’t think so, not really. I mean, if I hear a noise outside at night I’ll notice it, but I think most people would. It’s not like I’m moving around because I think something bad is about to happen.\n\nIt’s more like I just get up to do one thing, and then—well, like the other day I went to check if the back door was shut, and then I saw the mail on the table, and then I remembered I hadn’t watered the plant by the window. That plant’s been half-dead for years anyway, my wife used to say I either drowned it or forgot it completely.\n\nBut no, I don’t think I feel alert for danger exactly. I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Did this getting-up-and-going all day become noticeably worse after you stopped working?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say that’s when it really got noticeable. When I was working, I was always moving anyway, so it just seemed normal—like there was always another job to get to, somebody needing something, that sort of thing.\n\nBut since I stopped working, I still can’t really slow down. I’m up and down all day, going from one little thing to the next, like there’s still a crew waiting on me or I’m supposed to be somewhere. I’ll start to sit, then think of something in the garage, then the trash, then some little thing in the kitchen… it just keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Did anything traumatic happen around the time you stopped working?\n\nPatient: Traumatic? No, I don’t think so. I mean, stopping work was strange, sure—after doing it for so many years, suddenly your day doesn’t have the same shape to it.\n\nBut nothing like an accident or… I don’t know, something terrible happening right then. It was more just a change. I remember cleaning out my locker and finding old receipts and a pair of gloves I thought I’d lost, and then I brought all that junk home and it sat by the garage door for weeks.\n\nSo, no, not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Have being easily distracted and jumping from one task to another caused problems for you in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I suppose. I don’t like saying that, but yes.\n\nMy wife ended up sort of taking over the calendar, because I’d say I was going to be somewhere and then I’d get caught up doing something else, or I’d start one thing and forget the other thing completely. She checks behind me more than I’d like to admit.\n\nAnd I’ve had to apologize to the community center and the garden group a few times, too. I meant to bring something, or show up for something, and then… well, I didn’t follow through. The garden group had this whole thing with mulch one Saturday—funny how much people can argue about mulch—but anyway, yes, I let people down more than once.\n\nDoctor: Even when you try to relax, do you feel restless or unable to stay seated for very long?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Even if I’m trying to relax, like sitting through a quiet meeting or one of those church talks, I start feeling like I need to move around.\n\nI’ll shift in the seat, tap my foot, stretch my legs out, then pull them back in… that sort of thing. Sometimes I’m thinking, “Just sit still, you’re a grown man,” but then my back feels tight or my knee starts bouncing. Church pews don’t help either—never built for comfort, if you ask me.\n\nBut yes, even when I know I’m supposed to stay put, I get that feeling like I ought to get up and stretch or move around.\n\nDoctor: When you get like this, do other people tell you that you’re talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. My wife might say something if I get going, but I don’t know if that’s “more than usual” or just me telling a story badly.\n\nI mean, some people tell stories straight from A to B, and I’ll remember something about a neighbor’s dog or what happened at the hardware store, and then I’ve taken the scenic route. But as far as people saying, “You’re talking too much today,” no… I don’t really recall that being a regular thing.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually confident, like you could do things better than other people or take on more than was realistic?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I’ve had moments where I thought, “Oh, I can handle that,” and then it turned out to be more of a project than I expected. Like trying to fix something around the house and ending up with parts laid out everywhere—my wife still reminds me about a cabinet hinge I “definitely knew how to fix.”\n\nBut unusually confident, like I’m better than other people or can take on the world? No, I don’t think so. If anything, I usually second-guess myself more than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose or misplace الأشياء you need, like keys, papers, or tools?\n\nPatient: Oh yes, all the time. I spend a ridiculous amount of time hunting for my glasses, my keys, the tape measure… and I’ve got this little notebook I use for story ideas, and that disappears on me too.\n\nThe funny thing is, half the time I just had the thing in my hand a few minutes before. I’ll walk into another room, set it down somewhere “safe,” and then I’m checking the kitchen counter, the chair, the garage… My wife will say, “Did you look where you were just standing?” and darn if she isn’t usually right.\n\nDoctor: Do you repeatedly check things like locks, doors, or appliances even after you know you already checked them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not repeatedly, anyway. I mean, I’ll check the front door at night, sure, but I think most people do that. Sometimes I’ll stand there a second and wonder if I turned the porch light off, and then I’ll remember we started leaving it on because of that raccoon that kept getting into the trash—well, I guess that’s not really what you asked.\n\nNo, I don’t believe I’m going back over and over to check locks or the stove or things like that after I already know I checked them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

(Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n[S013] Quiet_Activity_Difficulty: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n[S014] Constant_Activity: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n[S015] Excessive_Talking: Often talks excessively.\n[S016] Blurting_Answers: Often blurts out an answer before a question has been completed.\n[S017] Waiting_Turn_Difficulty: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n[S018] Interrupting_Intruding: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n[S019] Delusions: Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence.  Subtypes: Persecutory Delusions, Referential Delusions, Grandiose Delusions, Erotomanic Delusions, Nihilistic Delusions, Somatic Delusions, Jealous Delusions\n[S020] Hallucinations: Perception-like experiences that occur without an external stimulus and are as vivid and clear as normal perception.  Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.  Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Now it’s just me at home, but my body still acts like I’ve got to keep moving.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [judge] ==========

Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"None\",\"name\":\"None\",\"description\":\"None\",\"reason\":\"The doctor is asking about excessive worry or generalized anxiety, which is not covered by the provided ADHD symptom profile items.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt. Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. I’ll start to sit, then think of something in the garage, then the trash, then some little thing in the kitchen… it just keeps going.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nTraumatic? No, I don’t think so. I mean, stopping work was strange, sure—after doing it for so many years, suddenly your day doesn’t have the same shape to it.\n\nBut nothing like an accident or… I don’t know, something terrible happening right then. It was more just a change. I remember cleaning out my locker and finding old receipts and a pair of gloves I thought I’d lost, and then I brought all that junk home and it sat by the garage door for weeks.\n\nSo, no, not that I can think of.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }

Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.
